Ὁ Κατὰ γὰρ τοῦτο καὶ ὁ μέγας Μάξιμος ἐν τοῖς πρὸς θαλάσσιον ἔλεγεν ὅτι τὰ πάθη ταῦτα, ἤγουν ἡδονὴ λύπη ἐπιθυμία καὶ τὰ λοιπὰ πάντα, τῇ φύσει τῶν ἀνθρώπων προηγουμένως οὐ συνετἑθησαν, ἀλλὰ διὰ τὴν τῆς τελειότητος ἔκπτωσιν ἐπεισῆλθε ταῦτα τῷ ἀλογωτέρῳ μέρει προσφυέντα τῆς φύσεως, δι’ ὧν ἀντὶ τῆς θείας καὶ μακαρίας εἰκόνος εὐθὺς ἅμα τῇ παραβάσει διαφανὴς καὶ ἐπίδηλος ἐν τῷ ἀνθρώπῳ γέγονεν ἡ τῶν ἀλόγων ζώων ὁμοίωσις. Ἔδει γὰρ τῆς ἀξίας τοῦ λόγου καλυφθείσης ὑφ’ ὧν γνωμικῶς ἐπεσπάσατο τῶν ἀλόγων γνωρισμάτων, ἐνδίκως τὴν φύσιν τῶν ἀνθρώπων κολάζεσθαι, σοφῶς εἰς συναίσθησιν τῆς λογικῆς μεγαλονοίας ἐλθεῖν οἰκονομοῦντος τοῦ Θεοῦ τὸν ἄνθρωπον. Πλὴν οὐκ ἀναγκαίως τῷ ὄντι κατέθησεν ἡμᾶς ὁ Θεὸς μετὰ τὴν παράβασιν ταῖς ἐπιθυμίαις ταύταις, ὑφ’ ὧν ἐπὶ τὰς βρώσεις καὶ πόσεις καὶ ἐπὶ ἀφροδίσια καταφερόμεθα. Ἐντεῦθεν γὰρ τοῖς γεννητοῖς ἡμῶν σώμασι τὸ γεννᾶσθαι τε καὶ τρέφεσθαι καὶ διαμένειν ἐπὶ πλεῖστον, καθ’ ὅσον ἂν ἑκάστῳ τοῦ σώματος ἡ σύστασις ἐπιδέχεται. Καὶ γὰρ ἐκ τεσσάρων αὐτὸ στοιχείων συγκείμενον ἐπακολουθοῦσαν ἐξ ἀνάγκης κέκτηται τὴν διάλυσιν. Σύνθεσις γὰρ ἀρχὴ διαστάσεως, ὡς ὁ θεολόγος φησὶ Γρηγόριος. Λέλυται οὖν ἐπὶ τισὶ μὲν τάχιον, ἐπὶ τισὶ δὲ βράδιον. Καὶ πείθου τῷ μεγάλῳ Κυρίλλῳ λέγοντι· «Ἔτασε δὲ μετὰ τὴν ἀπόπτωσιν ἕκαστον ὁ Θεὸς τὴν ἑαυτοῦ ζωὴν ἐπληροῦν ὡς ἂν ἔτυχε κράτεως. » Ἐπὶ γὰρ τούτῳ καὶ τὰ συστατικὰ τῆς ζωῆς ἡμῶν, καρδίαν δηλονότι ἐγκέφαλον ἧπαρ καὶ ὅσα τοιαῦτα, στεῤῥοῖς ἅπανταχόθεν ὀστέοις ἡ φύσις ἐφρούρησεν, ὥστε μὴ καὶ ταῖς τυχούσαις προφάσεσι καταθλαπτομένων αὐτῶν ἑτοίμως οὕτω τὸν θάνατον ἕπεσθαι. Ταύταις μέντοι ταῖς ἐπιθυμίαις καὶ ἡδονὴν ὥσπερ τι δέλεαρ ἐγκατέμιξεν, ὡς ἐντεῦθεν ἡμᾶς ἑτοίμως καθέλκεσθαι. Εἰ μὴ γὰρ τοῦτο ἦν, οὐκ ἂν ἐπὶ βρώσεις καὶ πόσεις ἐτρίχομεν, οὐκ ἂν ἀφροδισίοις ὁλωσχερῶς προσεκείμεθα. Εἰ δὲ μὴ τοῦτο, τάχα ἂν ἐξ ἀνάγκης τὸ γένος ἀπόλωλε τὸ ἀνθρώπινον. Κατὰ τοῦτο μὲν οὖν ἡ γανή, περίττωμα οἷον οὖσα, κατεπείγει καὶ ἀνιᾷ καὶ ἀναγκάζει τὸ ζῷον ὁρμᾶν ἐπὶ τὴν ἀπόκρισιν αὐτῆς, εὐμεθόδως καὶ τοῦτο τοῦ Θεοῦ συγχωρήσαντος ἕνεκα τῆς τοῦ γένους δισμονῆς. Ταῦτα δὲ Μάνης καὶ Οὐαλεντῖνος, ἠγνοηκότες, ὡς ἔοικε, κτίσμα τοῦ διαβόλου ἔλεγον εἶναι, ὑπ’ αὐτοῦ τε πυροῦσθαι καὶ πρὸς ἀφμίας ἀποκυλίεσθαι. Πλὴν οὐκ ἀφῆκε πρὸς συναφείας ἡμᾶς ὁ Θεὸς εἶναι πάντη κατωφερεῖς, οὐδὲ ζῆν ἀνεπαισθήτως εἴασεν ὡς τὰ ἄλογα. Ἧ γὰρ ἡδονὴ καὶ ἀΐδιαν σύνζευξεν καὶ σύμμικτον ταύτην ἐποίησεν, ὡς ἐκεῖθεν μὲν ἐπὶ τοῦ γένους αὔξησιν ἕλκεσθαι, ἐντεῦθεν δὲ ταλανίζειν ἑαυτούς, τῶν προτέρων ἐπιμεμνῆσθαι, καὶ τῆς ἁμαρτίας μέγα καταδὸν, δι’ ἣν τοῖς ἀνοήτοις παρασυνελήφθημεν κτήνεσι.


[223] {#pg.158.223}
Præter hæc etiam illud scire te volo, partem eam animi quæ appetendi facultatem continet in jecinore sedem suam obtinere, alteram qua irascendi vis continetur in corde, rationis denique domicilium in cerebro esse. Atque hanc ipsam rationis facultatem gubernator ille rerum universitatis propterea tibi donavit, ut ab irascibili facultate adjutus fortiter adversari brute pecuniæque concupiscentiæ possis. Norat enim dominus ille naturæ quantus esset ejus impetus. Et hanc appetendi vim sedem in jecinore suam habere Galenus etiam Tityi commemorata fabula probat, in libro quidem eo cui Diagnostices titulum fecit, jecur ejus ab aquila comedi, in altero autem libro singulari qui est de animi moribus, a vulturibus devorari tradens. Etenim Tityus hic, ut exteræ disciplinæ fabulis continentur, Latonam adamasse perhibetur, eamque ob causam ab illius liberis Apolline ac Diana supplicio affectus. Galeno quidem narrante hominem vel ab aquila vel a vulturibus ab-sumi, aliis vero tradentibus sagittis eum fuisse confixum, quæ quidem adhuc jecinori ejus inhærant. Ex quo intelligi non obscure potest appetentem animæ vim esse in jecinore sitam.
Atque hoc loco quid sim dicturus considerato. Cum rationis facultas imperium obtinet eique vis irascibilis paret, appetente debilitate submissaque se gerente, tum vero 112 nec intemperanter homines vivant, nec prædonum aut tyrannorum in morem, sed honesta plane justaque vita degitur. Hac de re dubitari non debet. Nam si res ita comparata non foret, et si corporis in eadem structura non hic affectionibus foret obnoxius, ille se superiorem iis declararet, nequaquam magnus ille Basilius Isaiam vatem enarrans quoddam a natura præcellere diceret aliis, et ipsius etiam carnis comparatione multis præstare, sive in scientiæ alicujus perceptione sive in cultu virtutis. Hinc videre est varios homines, temperantes et intemperantes, iracundos et mites, prudentes et stolidos, ut supra quoque monuimus. Atque hæc de conditione deteriore in melius commutari possunt, ac vicissim a bono in pejus licet deflectere, quemadmodum ex hisce Gregorii Theologi verbis perspicue intelligimus: «Satiest, inquit, corpori sua vitiositas. Cur humanæ materies amplior suppeditetur? cur huic belluæ nutrimentum largius? ut intractabilis scilicet fiat ac rationem violentia sua superet.» Animum igitur advertito. Quantam sibi vim afferant homines ii qui frugi quidem esse cupiunt vitamque angelicam imitari, verum facultatem appetentem in se validam sentiunt (etenim natura requirit ea quæ sua sunt; cujus voluntati atque motibus adversatur quisquis vitam agere castam instituit), quamque adeo grave sit repugnare naturæ velle, cui re-


[224] {#pg.158.224}
Περὶ τριῶν μερῶν τῆς ψυχῆς.
Πρὸς τούτοις εἰδέναι καὶ τοῦτό σε βούλομαι, ὅτι τὸ ἐπιθυμητικὸν μέρος τῆς ψυχῆς τῷ ἥπατι ἐγκαθίδρυται, τὸ δὲ θυμικὸν ἐν τῇ καρδίᾳ ποιεῖται τὴν οἴκησιν, τὸ δὲ λογικὸν ἐν αὐτῷ τῷ ἐγκεφάλῳ. Τοῦτο μὲν οὖν τὸ λογικὸν ἐχαρίσατό σοι ὁ τῶν ὅλων πρύτανις, ἵνα συμμάχῳ καὶ τῷ θυμικῷ χρώμενος, γενναίως ἀνθίστασαι κατὰ τῆς ἀλόγου καὶ βοσκημάτωδους ψυχῆς, τῆς ἐπιθυμητικῆς· δηλαδὴ οἶδε γὰρ ὁ τῆς φύσεως δεσπότης ὅση περ ἐκείνης ἐστὶν ἡ ὁρμή. "Ὅτι δὲ τὸ τῆς ψυχῆς ἐπιθυμητικὸν αὐτῷ τῷ ἥπατι ἐγκαθίδρυται, καὶ τὸν Τιτυὸν παράγει ὁ Γαληνὸς εἰς σύστασιν τούτου τοῦ λόγου, ἐν μὲν τῇ διαγνωστικῇ οὐτοῦ ὑπὸ ἀετοῦ λέγων αὐτοῦ κατεσθίεσθαι τὸ ἦπαρ, ἐν δὲ τῷ περὶ ἠθῶν ψυχῆς μονοβίβλῳ ὑπὸ γυπῶν. Ὁ γὰρ Τιτυὸς οὗτος κατὰ τὴν ἔξω μυθολογίαν ἐρασθῆναι λέγεται τῆς Λητοῦς, ἐφ' ᾗ καὶ παρὰ τῶν παίδων αὐτῆς Ἀπόλλωνός τε καὶ Ἀρτέμιδος κολάσει καθυποβληθῆναι, καὶ κατὰ μὲν τὸν Γαληνὸν ὑπὸ ἀετοῦ εἴτε καὶ γυπῶν τὸ ἦπαρ ἐσθίεσθαι, κατὰ δέ τινας ἄλλους τοξευθῆναι αὐτὸν κἀντεῦθεν ἔχειν τὰ βέλη ἐμπεπαρμένα τῷ ἥπατι. Εὔδηλον οὖν ἐντεῦθεν τῷ ἥπατι τὸ τῆς ψυχῆς ἐπιθυμητικὸν ἐστι.
Πρόσεχε λοιπόν. Ὅταν μὲν γὰρ τὸ λογιστικὸν ἄρχῃ, πειθόμενον μὲν ἑαυτῷ τὸ θυμοειδὲς ἔχον, ἀσθενὲς δὲ καὶ ταπεινὸν τὸ ἐπιθυμητικόν, οὔτε ἀκόλαστος ὁ τοιοῦτος βίος οὔτε οἷον οἱ λῃσταὶ βιοῦσι καὶ τύραννοι, ἀλλὰ ὁ μυὸς πάντη καὶ δίκαιος. Καὶ τούτου χάριν ἀμφιβάλλειν οὐ χρή. Εἰ μὴ γὰρ τοῦτο ἦν, εἰ μὴ ἐξ αὐτῆς τοῦ σώματος κατασκευῆς οὗτος μὲν ὑπὸ παθῶν ἥττητο, ἐκεῖνος δὲ πάλιν ἐγκρατὴς αὐτῶν κατεφαίνετο, οὐκ ἄν ὁ μέγας Βασίλειος τὸν προφήτην Ἠσαΐαν ἐξηγούμενος εἰσήγε τινὰς ἐκ φύσεως ἔχοντας τινὰ προτερήματα καὶ κατὰ τὴν σύγκρισιν τῆς σαρκὸς πολλῶν διαφέροντας εἰς ἀνάληψιν ἐπιστήμης ἢ ἄσκησιν ἀρετῆς. Ἐντεῦθεν οὖν καὶ σώφρονα ἴδοις ἄν καὶ ἀκόλαστον, θυμώδη καὶ πρᾶον, ἐχέφρονα καὶ μὴ τοιοῦτον, ὥσπερ δὴ καὶ τοῖς ἔμπροσθεν εἴρηται. Ταῦτα μὲν οὖν δύντεἄν τις ἐπὶ τὸ κρεῖττον μεταφέρειν ἀπὸ τοῦ χείρονος, καὶ ἐπὶ τὸ χεῖρον αὖ μετατρέπειν ἀπὸ τοῦ κρείττονος. Καὶ δῆλον ἐξ ὧν ὁ θεολόγος Γρηγόριος ἔλεγεν· «Ἀρκετὸν τῷ σώματι ἡ κακία αὐτου. Τί δὲ δεῖ τῇ φλογὶ πλείονος ὕλης, τῷ θηρίῳ δαψιλεστέρας τροφῆς; ἵνα δυσκάθεκτον γένηται καὶ τοῦ λογισμοῦ βαιότερον.» Πρόσεχε οὖν ὅσα βιάζουσιν ἑαυτοὺς οἱ θέλοντες μὲν σωφρονεῖν, ἔχοντες δὲ τὸ ἐπιθυμητικὸν ἰσχυρόν· ἡ μὲν γὰρ φύσις τὰ ἑαυτῆς ἐξ ἀνάγκης ἐπιζητεῖ, ὁ δὲ ἀγνεύειν αἱρούμενος τοῖς θελήμασι τῆς σαρκὸς ἀντιπίπτει καὶ βίον ἀγγελικὸν μιμεῖσθαι φιλονεικεῖ. Ὅσον δὲ βαρὺ τὸ πρὸς φύσιν μάχεσθαι, καὶ οἷον ἐντεῦθεν τὸ ἔπαυλον, ὁ τῆς Κλίμακος Ἰωάννης ὁ ἁγιώτατος ἐδήλωσεν οὕτως εἰπών· «Ὁ σῶμα νικήσας φύσιν ἐνίκησεν· ὁ δὲ φύσιν


[225] {#pg.158.225}
νικήσας ὑπὲρ φύσιν ἐγένετο· ὁ δὲ τοῦτο γενόμενος βραχύ τι παρ᾿ ἀγγέλους ἠλάττωται, ἵνα μὴ λέγω μηδέν. Πρόσεχε δὲ ἔτι καὶ σφόδρα βαρυαλγοῦσι καὶ ἄχθονται οἱ θέλοντες μὲν σωφρονεῖν, ἔχοντες δὲ τὸ ἐπιθυμητικὸν ἰσχυρὸν πολυπληθῆ τε τὴν γονήν, πρὸς ἔξοδον ἀεὶ ἐπειγομένου τοῦ ἐνοχλοῦντος αὐτοῖς· καὶ μὴ κατά τι ἀμφίβαλλε. Εἰ γὰρ ὁ Διογένης ἐκεῖνος ὁ Κυνικός, τὸ τῆς φύσεως βάρος οὐκ ἐνεγκών, καὶ ταῦτα τοσοῦτον ἀσκητικώτατος ὤν, γυναῖκα ζητῆσαί λέγεται ὥστε τὸ βαρύνον ἀποθέσθαι καὶ τὸ ἑαυτοῦ σῶμα κουφίσαι, τί χρὴ καὶ λέγειν ὅσων ὑπομένουσι κάματον οἱ σφοδρὸν μέν, ὡς εἴρηται, λαχόντες τὸ ἐπιθυμητικὸν, ἐν ἁγνείᾳ δὲ φυλάσσειν ἑαυτοὺς προθυμούμενοι; Ἐντεῦθεν, ἀγαπητέ, τῶν ὁσίων τινές, ἵνα τὴν φυσικὴν ἀποσβέσωσι πύρωσιν, καὶ γυμνοὶ τὸ σῶμα μέσον ὑδάτων διημερεύοντες ᾠφθησαν. Μηδεὶς λοιπὸν ὑπὸ μέσου προσφερέτω τὴν γένεσιν, μηδεὶς οὐκ ἀγαθοὺς ἀστέρων προφασιζέσθω σχηματισμούς. Ἀστέρων μὲν γὰρ κινήσεις, ἀνατολαί, καὶ δύσεις αὐτῶν, τοῖς προσήκουσι γίνεσθαι καιροῖς ᾠκονόμησεν ὁ σοφός· τὰ πάντα διευθετῶν, ὡς ἐντεῦθεν ἀέρος μετατρεπομένου καὶ ἔμβρυον καταῤῥηγνυμένων εὐκαίρως αὔξεσθαι τὰ φυτά. Καὶ γὰρ τῶν σοφῶν ὡς ἐν κύκλῳ διηνεκῶς χορηγουμένων ἀνελλιπὴς καὶ ἡ τῶν προσφερομένων τοὺς καρποὺς διαμένει φύσις, ὡς εὑρίσκεσθαι μὲν ἐκεῖνα διὰ τοὺς καρπούς, τοὺς δὲ καρποὺς διὰ τὰ ζῶα καὶ τὸν ἄνθρωπον. Οὐ γνῶμα· λοιπὸν οἱ ἀστέρες μετατρέπειν, οὐδὲ καθάπερ τι ἀνδράποδον τὸν κατ᾿ εἰκόνα Θεοῦ περιάγουσιν ἄνθρωπον. Οὐδὲ χρεὶς τοσοῦτον ἂν εὑρεθεῖεν ἄδικοι, τοὺς μὲν μοιχοὺς ποιοῦντες, τοὺς δὲ φονέα, καὶ ἁρπαγέας. Ἀλλὰ καὶ πρὸ τῶν ἀστέρων ὁ δημιουργγὸς αὐτῶν τὴν αἰτίαν ἀποφέρεται, εἴπερ τοιαῦτα κατεσκεύασεν & τὴν ἐπιφορὰν ἡμῖν ἐξ ἀνάγκης τῶν κακῶν ἐπιτίθησι, καθὰ δὴ καὶ πρὸς Φαῦστον ὁ κορυφαῖος ἔλεγε Πέτρος· γενέσει καὶ εἱμαρμένη τὸ πᾶν ἐπιτρέποντες, νισ esse causa factos. Quapropter animorum voluntates atque sententias sidera non mutant, nec hominem ad imaginem Dei factum mancipii cujusdam vilis instar hac illac ducunt. Non tam enim ipsa deprehenderentur injusta esse, quosdam adulteros efficientia, quosdam homicidas ac prædones, quam coram opifex, qui præcipuam in hac parte culpam mereretur, ob res ejusmodi conditas quæ necessario nos ad mala proclives redderent. Idcirco non abs re scripsit eximius ille Petrus ad Faustum, quosdam falso universum hoc natalitiis sideribus et fato committere.
Ὥρα λοιπὸν νουνεχῶς, καὶ μὴ ἐπὶ πλέον τοῖς λόγοις αὐτῶν θορυβοῦ. Εἰ γὰρ καὶ λέγουσιν ὅτι ἡ ὥρα καθ᾿ ἣν ἡ σύλληψίς τοῦ συλλαμβανομένου γίνεται, ἐκείνη καὶ τὸ πλαστουργούμενον, εἴτε ἄῤῥεν εἴτε θῆλυ, διατράττειν ἄριστα εἴωθεν, ἀλλ᾿ εὐθέως ἀνθυποφέρειν ἔχεις καὶ σύ, καὶ οὕτω λέγειν αὐτοῖς, ὡς εἴπερ ἐν μιᾷ ὥρᾳ καὶ τῇ αὐτῇ καταβολῇ τῶν διδύμων ἡ σύλληψις γίνεται, ἔπρεπε πάντως μὴ τὸ μὲν αὐτῶν ἄῤῥεν, τὸ δὲ θῆλυ, ἀλλ᾿ ἄῤῥενα καὶ ἄμφω κατὰ τὴν ἐπικρατοῦσαν ὥραν ἢ θήλεα ταυτὶ διαπλάτεσθαι. Οὕτω μὲν οὖν ἔδει μὴ τὸ μὲν αὐτῶν τάχιον τελευτᾷ, τὸ δὲ βράδιον, ἀλλ᾿ ὁμοῦ καὶ ἄμφω τῶν ὧδε μεθίστασθαι· κατὰ γὰρ ἐκείνους εἰπεῖν, ὁ αὐτὸς ἀστὴρ κἀκείνῳ καὶ τούτῳ τὴν ζωὴν ἐχαρίσατο. Ἀλλὰ καὶ ἐπί τισι παθήμασι καὶ συνώμασιν ὑφ᾿ ὧν ἁλίσκονται πολλάκις τὰ κατὰ μήτραν πηγνύμενα (τά τε γὰρ θήλεα σχῆμα πολλάκις ἀνδρῶν PATKOL. GR. CLVIII.
spondet ipsius etiam præmii magnitudo, sanctus ille vir Joannes auctor scalæ hisce verbis declarat: «Qui corpus vicit, naturam vicit; qui naturamvicit, supra naturam semet extulit; hoc qui consecutus est, paulum angelis est inferior, ne nihil ab iis differre dicam.» Nimirum scito gravibus eos molestiis premi qui casti esse desiderant, cum appetitum in se fortem experiantur et genitumagnam copiam, quæ negotii plurimum eis facessit ac nunquam non, ut emittatur, urget. Nihil de hoc ambigendum. Quippe si Diogenes ille Cyninus, cum onus illud naturæ nequiret amplius ferre, homo perpetuus et maximis exercitationibus ad abstinentiam condocefactus, mulierem aliquando quæsisse perhibetur, quo rem molestam sibi deponeret et corpus onere velut exeneso levaret, quantum illos sustinero laboris atque molestiarum existimandum est, in quibus a natura vehemens est appetitus impetus, cum nihilominus servare seipsos in continentia velint? Atque hanc ob causam, dilecte filii, quidam viri religiosi nudis corporibus totos dies in medias aquas semet abjecisse traduntur, quo inflammationem et æstum illum 113 naturalem restingerent. Itaque nemo posthac nativitatem suam in medium proferat, nemo inauspicatos siderum positus et inter se conformationes causetur. Nam sapiens ille Moderator universorum præclaro consilio instituit, ut suis temporibus certi siderum motus, ortus, occasus eveniant, quo commutato per has causas aere pluviisque delabentibus plantæ incrementum commode sumant. Cum enim alimenta quodam velut ambitu perpetuo suppeditata non desiderant, etiam illorum natura, qui fructus ipsos percipiunt, vel egere vel deficere nequit. De quo intelligimus sidera quidem cum motibus suis fructuum, fructus autem animalium et hominis esse causa factos. Quapropter animorum voluntates atque sententias sidera non mutant, nec hominem ad imaginem Dei factum mancipii cujusdam vilis instar hac illac ducunt. Non tam enim ipsa deprehenderentur injusta esse, quosdam adulteros efficientia, quosdam homicidas ac prædones, quam coram opifex, qui præcipuam in hac parte culpam mereretur, ob res ejusmodi conditas quæ necessario nos ad mala proclives redderent. Idcirco non abs re scripsit eximius ille Petrus ad Faustum, quosdam falso universum hoc natalitiis sideribus et fato committere.
Prudenter igitur hæc considerato, nec horum verbis plus æquo perturbari te sinito. Cum enim dicunt eam horam qua fetus conceptio fiat, fetum ipsum, tam marem quam femineum, optime formare, mox eis objicere vicissim potes quod si quidem una et eadem hora, una et eadem seminis dejectione gemeliorum conceptio fiat, omnino necesse sit alterum eorum non esse marem, alterum feminam, sed sed ambos mares vel feminas formari. Consimiliter eadem ratione sequeretur alterum eorum non debere citius exstingui, tardius alterum, sed utrumque rebus humanis pariter eximi. Nam, ut ipsi loquuntur, idem sidus tam huic quam illi vitam præbuit. Quin etiam eo progrediuntur, ut morbos quosdam et formas quæ fetibus in ipsa matrice nonnunquam accidunt (solent enim interdum femelle verendis in partibus virilem habere) B


[227] {#pg.158.227}


[228] {#pg.158.228}
περὶ τοῦ τὰ γεννητικὰ φέρουσι μόρια, καὶ τοὐναντίον αὖθις τὰ ἄρρενα), τοὺς ἀστέρας αἴτιον· ἤγηνται. Ταύτῃ τοι καὶ ἑρμαφροδίτους τοὺς τοιούτους ἐπονομάζουσι. Κομψεύονται γάρ, ἐπειδὰν ὁ Ἑρμῆς καὶ ἡ Ἀφροδίτη εὑρεθεῖεν ὁμόκεντροι, συνόντος αὐτοῖς τοῦ Κρόνου, πρὸς δὲ καὶ σχηματιζομένου τοῦ Ἄρεως, αἴτιοι τηνικαῦτα καὶ τῶν παρὰ φύσιν παθῶν τοῖς τικτομένοις καθίστανται. Ἀλλ’ οὐκ ἔστι τοῦτο, οὐκ ἔστιν· ἡ γὰρ διαπλάττουσα τὸ ἔμβρυον φύσις αἰτία πολλάκις τῶν τοιούτων εὑρίσκεται, καθὼς ἄνωθεν εἴρηται. Ἀλλὰ καὶ ὁ παρ’ αὐτοῖς τοῖς Ἕλλησι τιμώμενος Ἑρμῆς Ἀμμωνι τῷ σαφῷ ἐπιστέλλων αὐτοὺς αἰτιᾶται τοὺς ἀστέρας ἐπί τε τοῖς μαινομένοις καὶ τὸ φρόνημα κεκτημένοις παράφορον ὡς αἰτίους δῆθεν τοιούτων παθῶν. Φησὶ γὰρ ὧδε· ἐπειδὰν ἐξ ἀγαθοῦ σχήματος ὁ τοῦ ἐγκεφάλου κυριεύων ἀστὴρ κατοπτεύει τὸ πλαστουργούμενον, κἀκεῖθεν ἀκτῖνα βάλλει τῷ ἐγκεφάλῳ τοῦ πηγνυμένῳ, καλῶς ἕξουσιν αἱ ἡγεμονικαὶ δυνάμεις τῷ πλαττομένῳ τηνικαῦτα ἐμβρύῳ· εἰ δὲ κακούμενος, ὁπωσδήποτε τύχῃ ὁ τοιοῦτος ἀστὴρ, τῶν ἐναντίων ἔσται πάντως ποιητικός. Καὶ οὕτω μὲν οἱ τοῖς μετεώροις προσέχοντες καὶ φρενοῦσι περὶ τούτων καὶ λέγουσιν (24). Σὺ δέ, ἀγαπητέ, μὴ οὕτως, ἀλλὰ μοχθηροῖς τισι χυμοῖς κατακρατεῖσθαι τὸν ἐγκέφαλον πίστευε, ἡνίκα δὲ θεάσῃ τινὰ παρακοπῇ φρενῶν συνεχόμενον· ὁ γὰρ ἐγκέφαλος, ὄργανόν ἐστι τοῦ νοός· ταύτῃ τοι καὶ κατὰ τὴν ὀργάνου διάθεσιν καὶ ὁ τεχνίτης εἴωθε ἐνεργεῖν. Ἐπὶ γὰρ τούτῳ καὶ βοτάνας ἀκεσωδύνους παρήγαγεν ὁ Θεός, καὶ σοφίας ἰατρικῆς τοὺς ἀνθρώπους ἐνέπλησεν· ἐντῦθεν γὰρ καὶ κεφαλὴν καθαιρομένην ἴδῃ τις, καὶ πάθη ἄλλα τινὰ θεραπείας ἀξιούμενα φαρμάκων καταλλήλων ἐπαγωγῆς. Μὴ οὖν συναρπάζου τοῖς λόγοις αὐτῶν· ὅτι μηδὲ καθ’ εἱμαρμένην, ὡς εἴρηται, τὰ πάθη ταῖς ἀνθρώποις ἐμπίπτουσιν. Τί δὲ τὸ τῆς εἱμαρμένης ἐστὶ σημαινόμενον; Ὦ, ἐκεῖνοι φασίν, ἡ ἀπὸ τῆς καθ’ εἱρμὸν τῶν οὐρανίων σωμάτων κινήσεως· ἀπαράβατος ἔκβασις, ἣν δὴ καὶ πάντων ὁ τῶν ἁγίων χορὸς πόῤῥω που ῥίπτει καὶ ἀποπέμπεται. Τίνος ἕνεκεν; Ὅτι πολὺ μὲν τὸ ἀνόητον κέκτηται, πολλαπλάσιον δὲ τὸ ἀσεβές, ὡς, ὁ μέγας, φησὶ Βασίλειος.
Φυσικῶς μὲν οὖν, ὡς προείρηται, καὶ διοικεῖσθαι τὸ ἡμέτερον σῶμα καὶ διεξάγεσθαι συγκεχώρηται πάντως παρ’ αὐτοῦ τοῦ Δεσπότου τῆς φύσεως. Εἰ μή γὰρ τοῦτο ἦν, οὐκ ἦν ἡ τῆς ζωῆς ἡμῶν σύστασις· καὶ ἀέρος ἔχρηζε καθαροῦ καὶ ὑδάτων διεειδῶν τόπου τε ὑγιεινοῦ καὶ καταλλήλων ὡσαύτως βρωτῶν καὶ ποτῶν. Ἀλλ’ ὅρα τὴν τοῦ Θεοῦ σοφίαν, ἀγαπητέ, εἴ γε καὶ φυσιολογίας μικρὸν ἅψασθαι. Ἵνα γὰρ μὴ τὰ καιριώτατα τοῦ σώματος ἡμῶν μόρια καὶ ταῖς τυχούσαις προφάσεσι καταθλάπτωνται, πολυτρόπως, ὡς προείπομεν, καὶ πολυστιδῶς ἠσφαλίσατο. Αὐτίκα γὰρ τῇ κεφαλῇ ἡμῶν, ὅτε δὴ τὸν ἐγκέφαλον αὐτὸν περιέχουσαν ἐν ᾧ τὰ
speciem, ac vice versa mares femineam) astris A auctoribus tribuant. Vocant ejusmodi fetus, de quibus modo loquebamur, hermaphroditos. Nam perbelle fingunt Mercurium (qui Græcis Hermes est) et Venerem (quæ Aphrodite) in iisdem centris conjunctos. Saturno etiam accedente certaque conformatione aeris, fetus quosdam præter naturam affectos producere. Sed enim hoc minime verum est. Nam ipsa fetus formatrix 114 natura nonnunquam ejusmodi efficit, quemadmodum est supra demonstratum. Hérmes quidem ille philosophus, quem Græci tanto habent in honore, scriptis ad sapientem Ammonem litteris, morborum in iis causas qui furore corripiuntur et mente sunt alienati, ad sidera refert. Cum enim, inquit, sidus illud in cujus potestate cerebrum est, fetum bono B quodam aspectu intuetur, ejusque cerebrum in ipsa formatione radiis ejusmodi collustrat, omnino fit ut facultates animi principes in tali fetu præclare comparatæ sint. At si quomodocunque sidus ipsum male afficiatur, etiam diversa erit et contraria priori jus effectio. Hæc in modo spectatores illi sublimium sentiunt atque loquuntur. Eorum tu opinionem rejicito, ac pravis quibusdam humoribus cerebrum ejus repletum esse credito, quem animo alienatum conspexeris. Nam cerebrum mentis est instrumentum, ideoque pro ipsius instrumenti affectione mens etiam, velut artifex, agere consuevit. Propterea Deus herbas quasdam condidit quibus variis doloribus atque morbis C medemur, et hominibus medicam sapientiam abunde largitus est. Hine et cerebrum purgari videmus, et morbos alios, qui curationem requirunt, convenientium medicamentorum adhibitione sanari. Non igitur eorum verbis decipi te patitor. Ac ne fato quidem, ceu dictum est, hi morbi hominibus accidunt. Quid enim aliud sibi fati vult vocabulum, de ipsorum sententia, quam quod, sit eventus quidam inevitabilis, ortus ex serie perpetua motus cœlestium corporum? Quod ipsum totus ille sanctorum virorum cœtus longissime rejicit. Quamobrem istue? Quia multum amentiæ stoliditatisque continet, plus impietatis, ut magnus ille Basilius loquitur.
Naturaliter quidem, ceu diximus, gubernari re- D gique corpus nostrum ipse rerum Dominus voluit; absque quo foret, nec aere puro nec acquis pellucidis nec loco salubri nec sumptis per vices esculentis ac poculentis vita nostra indigeret. Enimvero, chare fili, fac divinam mihi sapientiam intueare, siquidem nonnihil de rerum natura nobis est commentandum. Etenim ne maxime principales necessariæque corporis nostri partes quibusvis de causis læderentur, caput nostrum, uti quod cerebrum continet quo vita nostra tota nititur, tum capillis tum ossibus præter illos solidis durisque munivit. Cerebrum ipsum natura tenerum fecit, quandoquidem


[229] {#pg.158.229}
τῆς ζωῆς ἡμῶν περιείληπται, καὶ θρὶξ κατώχυρωσε καὶ ὀστέοις πρὸ τούτων στερροῖς. Ἀπαλὴν δὲ ἡ φύσις τοῦτον ἐποίησεν, ἐπειδὴ ταῖς αἰσθήσεσι πάσαις αὐτὸς χορηγεῖ τὰς πηγάς. Ἵνα δὲ μὴ τῇ στερρότητι τῶν ὀστέων συντριβόμενος περὶ τὴν οἰκείαν βλάπτηται φύσιν, ἁπαλὸς καὶ γάρ, δυσὶ μήνιγξιν αὐτὸν περιέλαβε, καὶ ταύταις; οὐχ ἁπλῶς, ἀλλ᾿ ἁπαλῇ μὲν οὖσα ἔσωθεν διά γε τὴν τοῦ ἐγκεφάλου ἀνάπαυσιν, στερροτέρᾳ δὲ πάλιν τῇ ἔξωθεν, ὡς ἐντεῦθεν ἄμφω φυλάττεσθαι· οὕτω μὲν οὖν καὶ ἐπὶ τῆς καρδίας ἐποίησε, καθὰ δὴ καὶ ὁ χρυσοῤῥήμων Ἰωάννης φησὶν, ὀστέοις μὲν περικυκλώσας, πραθεὶς δὲ αὐτῇ καθάπερ ἁπαλὸν στρῶμα τὸν πνεύμονα, ἵνα μὴ τῇ ἀεικινησίᾳ καὶ αὕτη τοῖς ὀστέοις συντριβῆται. Ἀλλὰ πολλαχόθεν ἔχειν ῥαφὰς πολλῆς ἀσφαλείας ὑπόθεσις· ἐξετμίζεται γὰρ ἐκεῖθεν αὐτός, ὥστε μὴ συμπνίγεσθαι ὑπὸ τῶν ἀναφερομένων ἀτμῶν. Πληγὴ τε ἐξ τε ἐπενεχθεῖσα ποτὲ, οὐχ ὁλοσχερῶς ἡ βλάβη γίνεται· εἰ μὲν γὰρ ἓν καὶ συνεχὲς ἦν τὸ ὀστοῦν, καὶ μία κατενεχθεῖσα πληγὴ τὸ πᾶν ἐλυμήνατο, εἰς πολλὰ δὲ νυνὶ διαιρουμένῳ γενέσθαι τοῦτο πάντως ἀμήχανον. Εἰ γὰρ καὶ συμβαίη τρωθῆναι μέρος ἓν, ἀνάγκη πᾶσα τῇ κατ᾿ ἐκεῖνο τὸ μέρος κείμενον ὀστοῦν παραβλάπτεσθαι, ἀσινῆ δὲ τὰ λοιπὰ πάντα διαφυλάττεσθαι, τοῦ συνεχοῦς τῆς πληγῆς· διακοπτομένου τῇ τῶν ὀστέων διαιρέσει. Περὶ δὲ τῶν τῆς κεφαλῆς περιττωμάτων τί χρὴ καὶ λέγειν, εἴ γε τὰς ἐκκρίσεις αὐτῶν, ὡς ὁρᾷς, ἡ ἔσφησις πεπίστευται; Ἐπειδὴ γὰρ τοὺς κάτωθεν ἀναπεμπομένους ἀτμοὺς ὡς ὑπερκειμένη αὕτη τοῦ σώματος· ὑποδέχεσθαι εἴωθεν, ἡ δὲ τούτων πλημμύρα πολλὴν ἂν εἰργάσατο τῷ ἐγκεφάλῳ τὴν λώβην, οὐδὲ τοῦτου κατημέλησεν ὁ τῶν ἁπάντων ποιητὴς, ἀλλὰ πόρους τινὰς ἐν τοῖς ὀστέοις ἐργασάμενος τὰ ὑγρὰ καὶ παχέα τῶν περιττωμάτων ἐπὶ τὴν ὑπερῴαν καὶ τοὺς μυκτῆρας ὠδήγησε, καὶ δύο τούτοις ἐκκρίσεων ἀπένειμε μόρια, ἵνα συντόμως τὸ βλαβερρὸν ἐκφορῆται. Τὸ δέ γε ἀτμῶδες περίττωμα διὰ τῶν τοῦ κρανίου ῥαφῶν ἄνωθεν ἐξωθεῖται. Μὴ θαύμαζε· εἰ γε ψυχρός ἐστιν ὁ ἐγκέφαλος φύσει, καὶ ὅτι μείζονα τοῦτον ὁ ἄνθρωπος ἢ τὰ λοιπὰ ζῶα κέκτηται. Τὴν γὰρ ἐν τῇ καρδίᾳ θερμότητα τε καὶ ζέσιν χωρεῖν ἐπὶ τὸ εὔκρατον ἐντεῦθεν ὁ Ἀριστοτέλης φησὶν, αὐτὸν δὲ αὖ τὸν ἐγκέφαλον μὴ ὑπερθερμαίνεσθαι τῇ ἀναφερομένῃ θερμότητι. Ὥρα δὲ νουνεχῶς· ἐπειδὴ γὰρ ἡ τοιαύτη θερμότης μείζων ἐστὶ, μείζων ἄρα καὶ ἡ ἐξ ἐγκεφάλου ψυχρότης τε ὁμοῦ καὶ ὑγρότης ἀντίκειται, ἧς ὑγρότητος καὶ ὀψιαϊτάτα πήγνυται τὰ περὶ τὴν κεφαλὴν ὀστοῦν, ὃ δὴ καὶ βρέγμα καλοῦσί τινες. Ἀλλὰ καὶ ῥαφὰς πλείους ὁ ἄνθρωπος κέκτηται, καὶ μᾶλλον ὁ ἀνήρ. Τίνος ἕνεκεν; Ὅτι καὶ πλείων ὁ ἐγκέφαλος αὐτῷ πρόσεστιν· ἵνα γοῦν ἔμπνους ὁ τόπος ᾖ, πλειόνων ὁ ἀνὴρ ἐδεήθη ῥαφῶν. Καὶ ξηρὸν μὲν οὖν τοιοῦτος καὶ ἀπέριττος ὢν συνετόν τινα ποιεῖ καὶ νηφάλιον, κάθυγρος δὲ ὢν τἀναντία ποιεῖ, νωθρὸν δηλονότι καὶ ὑπνώδη καὶ ῥάθυμον. Καὶ πρόςσχες, εἰ βούλει, τῷ σοφῷ Ἡρακλείτῳ· ξηροτέρη ψυχὴ


[230] {#pg.158.230}
cunctis sensibus suos quasi fontes suppeditat. Rursum ne tam tenera substantia propter ossium daritiem collisa velut attereretur ac detrimentum caperet, duabus eam membranis 115 est complexa, iisque non unius generis, sed interiore quidem molli ob cerebri quietem, exteriore vero solidiori, quo per illam utriusque saluti consuleretur. Idem in corde factum, Joanne Chrysostomo teste, quod ossibus quidem cinxit Deus; sed nihilominus eidem, quasi teneram quamdam vestem stragulam, pulmonem adjunxit, ne et ipsum ad ossa propter perpetuitatem motus allideretur. Quin illud etiam, quod continuum et unum sit os quo cerebrum tegitur, sed omni ex parte suturas habeat, quam præclare consultum sanitati nostræ sit, argumento est. Nam per eas vapores suos emittit, ne per halitus sursum contendentes suffocetur; et eadem ratione consequimur ut illata forte cerebro plaga non totum lædat. Quippe si os continuum et unum foret, ab uno vulnere totum læderetur. Nunc cum in ossa plura divisum sit, fieri hoc omnino nequit. Nam si maxime pars una vulneretur, necesse est illud os lædi quod illa est in parte situm, cum illæsa cætera maneant, vulneris continuitate per ossium divisionem interrupta. Quid de-capitis excrementis dicam? quorum egestioni nares sunt, ut vides, destinatæ. Cum enim caput tanquam supremum in corpore locum consecutum vapores deorsum ascendentes excipere soleat, quorum redundantia cerebro plurimum detrimenti allatura erat, ne id quidem opifex ille rerum negligere voluit, sed quibusdam meatibus effectis in ipsis ossibus excrementa humida et crassa in palatum ac nares deduxit, quas ipsas quoque duas in partes divisit, quibus excretio fiat, ut compendiaria quasi via breviter noxia egerantur et excrementia sursum exhalantia per cranii suturas sursum expellantur. Neque cerebri naturam esse frigidam miraberis, nec quod in homine cerebrum sit amplius quam in animalibus cæteris. Nam hinc tradit Aristoteles calorem ebullitionemque cordis temperatiorem effici, et ipsum cerebrum propter calorem sursum tendentem non nimium effervescere. Quod sane si fieret, non parum effectiones ejus impedirentur. Atque hoc loco mentem mihi velim adhibeas: Quia magnus est cordis calor, idcirco etiam major illi frigidatis cerebri et humiditas opponitur; cujus humiditatis causa perquam serò capitis os consolidatur, quod bregma nonnulli vocant. Et suturas capitis, ceu diximus, homo plures habet, præsertim vir. Quamobrem istue? Quia cerebrum in eo quoque majus est. Ut igitur hic locus fetoris expers esset maxime, vir pluribus suturis egebat pro ipsius nimirum cerebri amplitudine. 116 Quodsi cerebrum quis siccum minimeque gravatum ab excrementis habeat, prudens atque sobrius evadit, cum ejusdem humiditas contrarium quiddam efficiat, hominem videlicet desidem, somnolentum, ignavum. Licet hoc de sapientis Heracliti


[231] {#pg.158.231}
verbis intelligas, qui animam sicciorem dixit esse  sapientiorem. Nam in cerebris humidis spiritus animalis veluti submersus, ut et in feminis est vide, præclarissime suas effectiones absolvere nequit. Idcirco divinus ille Cyrillus sexum femineum dimidia parte virorum indoli cedere dixit. Atque hæc de statu conditioneque cerebri naturali dicta sunto. Cum autem plus æquo siccescit, necesse est hominem lædi et affligi. Nam vehementes quædam temperamenti justi perturbationes parijum ipsarum effectiones quasi retundere consueverunt. Itaque fieri plerumque solet, ut vel jejunia vel vigiliæ naturalem cerebri siccitatem plus æquo augentes hujusmodi morbos gignant. Estque propterea necessarium ut homines in hunc modum affecti vietus ratione aliquanto humidiore utantur; nam orti  de siccitate morbi per humiditatem curantur. Rursum si ex morbo quodam plus æquo sit humidum cerebrum, subsequi rerum oblivio et infatuatio quædam solet, eaque tanta ut sæpenumero tam artium quam litterarum suique adeo nominis homo prorsus obliviscatur. Tribus nimirum membranis cerebrum distinguitur, ac ventriculus quidem ejus anterior facultatis informantis et imaginantis est instrumentum; medius vero cogitantis, posticus denique recordantis. Etenim primo rem hanc aut illam informatione quadam concipimus; deinde de informato rationamur, quid et quotuplex a natura sit, atque ita in memoriam tandem recondimus. Et hæc quidem accipienda de iis sunt qui recte affecti  sunt atque sani. Quodsi vel unum aliquod horum morbo quodam quocunque pacto afficiatur, tunc ejus partis ratione anima pure nequit efficax esse. Hæc tibi, dilecte fili, monendo subjicio, ne siderum inter se conformationes et aspectus causeris, cum naturalibus hujusmodi quædam rationibus evenire consueverint.

σοφωτέρην. Ἐπὶ γὰρ τῶν ὑγρῶν κατακλυζόμενον οἱονεὶ τὸ ψυχικὸν πνεῦμα, καθάπερ καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν γυναικῶν ἐστιν ἰδεῖν, ἐνεργεῖν ἀρίστως οὐ δύναται. Κατὰ γὰρ τοῦτο καὶ τὴν ἀνδρῶν εὐφυΐας καὶ ἐξ ἡμισείας ἀπολείπεσθαι τὸ θῆλυ φησιν ὁ θειότατος Κύριλλος. Καὶ οὕτω μὲν ὁ λόγος περὶ τῶν κατὰ φύσιν ἐχόντων διέξεισιν. Ἡνίκα δὲ ξηρότερος ἑαυτοῦ καὶ πλέον τοῦ δέοντος ὁ ἐγκέφαλος γένηται, παραπαίειν ἐξ ἀνάγκης ποιεῖ τὸν ἄνθρωπον. Αἱ μεγάλαι γὰρ δυσκρασίαι τὰς ἐνεργείας τῶν μορίων ἀμβλύνειν εἰώθασιν. Ὅθεν ἡ ἀσιτία πολλάκις, ναὶ μὴν καὶ ἡ ἀγρυπνία, τὴν κατὰ φύσιν ἐπεκτείνουσα τοῦ ἐγκεφάλου ξηρότητα, ποιεῖν εἴωθε τοιαῦτα συμπτώματα. Κατὰ γὰρ τοῦτο καὶ ὑγροτέρᾳ διαίτῃ κεχρῆσθαι τοὺς οὕτω διατεθέντας ἐπάναγκες· τὰ γὰρ διὰ ξηρότητα πάθη τῇ ὑγρότητι θεραπεύονται: (25). Ψυχρότερος δὲ πάλιν ἑαυτοῦ τοῦτο γεγονὼς ὁ ἐγκέφαλος καὶ λήθην ἐπάγει καὶ μώρωσιν, κατὰ τοσοῦτον δὲ ὥστε καὶ τεχνῶν καὶ γραμμάτων καὶ τοῦ ὀνόματος πολλάκις αὐτοῦ μετὰ τὴν ὑγείαν ἐπιλελῆσθαι τὸν ἄνθρωπον. Τρισὶ καὶ ὑμέσι διῄρηται ὁ ἐγκέφαλος. Καὶ ἡ μὲν ἡ ἐμπρόσθιος αὐτοῦ κοιλία ὄργανόν ἐστι τοῦ φανταστικοῦ, ἡ δὲ μέση κοιλία ὄργανόν ἐστι τοῦ διανοητικοῦ, καὶ ἡ ἑτέρα πάλιν τοῦ μνημονευτικοῦ. Πρότερον μὲν γὰρ φανταζόμεθα τόδε τὸ πρᾶγμα· μετὰ ταῦτα διαλογιζόμεθα τὸ φαντασθὲν, τί τε καὶ ποταπὸν αὐτὸ τὴν φύσιν ἐστίν· εἶθ᾿ οὕτως τῇ μνήμῃ τὸ τοιοῦτον ἐναποτίθεμεν. Καὶ ταῦτα μὲν ἐπὶ τῶν ἐχόντων καλῶς· τε καὶ ὑγιῶς· ἀλλ᾿ ἐπειδὰν ἕν ἐξ αὐτῶν πάθει τινὶ ὁπωσδήποτε περιπέσῃ, τότε κατ᾿ ἐκεῖνο τὰ μέρη ἡ ψυχὴ καθαρῶς ἐνεργεῖν οὐ δύναται. Ταῦτα δὲ, τέκνον, ὑποτίθεμαί σοι, ὅπως μὴ τοὺς τῶν ἀστέρων σχημάτισμοὺς αἰτιάσῃ καλυποβάλλῃς, εἴ γε φυσικοῖς τισι λόγοις τὰ τοιαῦτα γίνεσθαι εἴωθε.
earth, He left him in that same dominion. For man, by the reason with which he is endowed, even today holds all things in his power. For although we see wild beasts sometimes rushing upon man according to their own impulse, we should not marvel at this to such an extent, since it happens by divine providence, so that in this way we may be reminded, even against our will, of the sin for which we were condemned to fear and tremble as though beneath a yoke. That man was appointed by God as the ruler of all things upon the earth is evident even from his form; for, as you see, man’s form is upright, reaching toward heaven and looking upward. All these things, as the Nyssen says, seem to be marks of authority and to signify royal dignity; for the fact that man alone among all existing things is such, while all other creatures have their bodies inclined toward the earth, clearly shows the difference in his status. Observe, therefore, beloved, the beginning that He allowed to exist. For by that which is present in him...
Pay attention, beloved. Wishing to honor man, God plants a paradise first, and only then places him within it, even though Pyrrhus the chronicler writes that Adam entered Paradise after forty days. But I do not know where Adam was dwelling before, if he spent forty days outside of Paradise. Now the golden-tongued John [Chrysostom] casts this suspicion aside, saying: "It was possible for Adam to dwell even outside Paradise. But those places were not marked out and destined for the habitation of man, but for the beasts of burden and all the wild animals of the earth. Paradise, however, was a royal dwelling place for them; for this reason, God led the animals to Adam as beings separated from him." If these things are so, do not offer your ears to Pyrrhus, but to this golden-tongued orator, for he is truly a disciple of the all-holy Spirit.
Following this, the text says: "Let us make for him a helper according to him," that is, similar to him in all things and of the same nature, but not similar to the remaining animals, because they do not happen to share the same nature with him. But here, Manes called God a liar, and Aquila did the same; for in their Greek version they interpreted the phrase "according to him" in a way contrary to Him, because perhaps this helper caused Adam to transgress, and hence the helper became a conspirator. And these are the opinions of such men; but according to the most divine Cyril and John Damascene, this helper was named for Adam for the sake of the constitution of the human race. Therefore, God casts a deep sleep upon Adam, and extracting one of his ribs, He builds the woman. And Adam, having beheld her later, most prophetically said: "This is now bone of my bones." For not even the woman, even still the same...
And the Evangelist, making this clear, said that even when he had taken the morsel, he went out; and it was night. He departs, therefore, to those with whom he had agreed to deliver up the Lord. And the Lord, having settled the final matters with his disciples, goes then to a place which the traitor also knew, who immediately, having taken the cohort from the chief priests and the officers of the Pharisees, comes there with lanterns and torches. He questions them, therefore; they fall to the ground. Peter cuts off the ear of Malchus, even if he was not able to cut off his head; for this was indeed what he was striving to do. Then, at that very moment, the Lord said to him, “Dost thou think that I cannot now beseech my Father, and He will provide for me more than twelve legions of angels? How then shall the Scriptures be fulfilled, that it must be so?” The Savior said these things to the disciples, striking fear into them and persuading them from that point on not to meddle curiously with any of the things taking place. For if, he says, this is pleasing to the Scriptures—that I must suffer, namely—how do you oppose, using swords? The cup which the Father has given me, shall I not drink it? Having therefore bound Jesus, they led Him away to Annas first, and there He is judged. He is questioned concerning His teaching and His disciples. He answers as is fitting. He receives a blow to the face, and He says, “If I have spoken evil, bear witness of the evil,” and the rest. From there, therefore, He is led to Caiaphas, then to Pilate, it being morning. Before the cock crows, He is led to Caiaphas; but in the morning, to Pilate. The Evangelist shows that for the entire half of the night, being questioned by Caiaphas, He was convicted of nothing; for which reason he also sent Him on to Pilate. And Mark, discussing the matter of the cock-crow, speaks thus: “Before the cock crow twice, thou shalt thrice deny me” (for when he denied once, the cock crowed the first time; but when he denied the third time, it crowed the second), explaining more accurately the weakness of Peter, having learned it from the teacher himself, that he was also a pupil of his. But Matthew, having said, “Before the cock crow, thou shalt thrice deny me,” indicated the whole of the cock-crowing—that is, the first, which occurs through different vocalizations, just as the second cock-crowing occurs, and the third. Therefore, by Matthew saying “Before the cock crow,” thou shalt thrice deny me, he was saying, as it were, that even before the first cock-crowing reached its end, thou shalt thrice deny me. After, however, the Lord was judged by Pilate, His back is scourged with braided thongs; for such is also the meaning of the word *flagellum* (scourge). After that, He is delivered up to be crucified. And all these things have been brought about by the unseasonable cowardice of Pilate; for the Jews...
ANNALS PART III. FROM THE REIGN OF CAESAR UNTIL CONSTANTINE THE GREAT Maccabaeus, who, moved by a certain divine zeal, slew the royal commander and performed countless other glorious deeds. But King Antiochus, having set out against Persia, and returning in dishonor, seized by a most grievous disease, recognized his state, saying: "It is just that I suffer for those things which I have committed against Jerusalem." And casting forth worms, he breathed out his soul. After him, his son Antiochus Eupator, during whose reign Judas Maccabaeus, having routed the commanders of Antiochus, stirred up Antiochus against himself with an infinite multitude and fifty-five elephants, upon which were wooden towers, each containing thirty-two armed soldiers. Then, indeed, then Judas performed great deeds, so as even to receive the high priesthood from the people. During his time, Jesus, the son of Sirach, having composed the all-virtuous work, was known to the Jews. The affairs of the Romans were previously conducted by consuls for 464 years until Julius Caesar, the "Caesar" — that is, the one not born by natural birth; for his mother having died in the ninth month of her pregnancy, they cut her open and brought him forth, whence he was called Caesar, from whom all the Roman emperors were called Caesars. He was dictator for ten years. *PART III. FROM THE REIGN OF CAESAR UNTIL CONSTANTINE THE GREAT.* Joannes Meursius, a Dutchman, a man excellently deserving of the literary republic, especially in publishing Greek writers—at Leiden in the year 1618, first published and translated into the Latin language the *Roman History* of Theodore Metochites from Julius Caesar to Constantine the Great, adding very brief notes, with a preface concerning Theodore Metochites himself, whose deeds he pursues, and Nicephorus Gregoras’s "Nenia" or Funeral Oration upon the same. But the learned man was deceived in his opinion, and led many into error, whether deluded by some little Greek who sold this fragment of our Glykas to him, or to the Reverend Father Andreas Schottus (from whom he confesses to have received it) as if it had been compiled by Theodore Metochites; or whether the name of the possessor, which the title "Metochitov" displayed, transitioned into the appellation of the true author, which in this case was missing; or for whatever other reason. Therefore, the book of the singular *Roman History* is nothing other than the third book of Michael Glykas, and that not entirely complete—namely, from page 204 of this Royal edition to 245, where the unhappy death of Galerius Maximianus is described. Concerning what else may be lacking in our work, we shall diligently compare the edition of Joannes Meursius with other manuscript codices which we have used thus far, and we shall attach his notes and emendations, designated by the capital letters JM. Roman affairs were administered by consuls for 464 years-—564 years, according to Constantinus Manasses, at PG 157. The Alexandrian Chronicle holds for 393 years. To our Theodore (correct this, reader, and replace Theodore with Glykas), Cedrenus agrees, and he is the author of other things as well. Florus counts up to Augustus. Indeed, the opinion of Manasses is closer, which is confirmed by the Capitoline Fasti, from which it is thought that from the first consuls to the monarchy of Julius were 460 years—namely, as I am accustomed to think, from the year of the City 245, in which Lucius Junius Brutus and Lucius Tarquinius Collatinus first entered the consulship, up to the year 705 of the same Rome, in which. Julius Caesar, created Dictator by Marcus Lepidus, prefect of the City, abdicated the dictatorship after the eleventh day, when he had been declared consul for the following year. On the same page, write for "eight" [in the text]. In the codex JM, mistakenly for "Ibidem" [it says] "eight." Leunclavius in his manuscript seems to have read "12," and thus translated "12 years," from which Joannes Meursius wrote this note: "Glykas [counts] 12 years, Cedrenus counts 4 years, 7 months," from which the Sicilian Fasti do not deviate. But instead of our Metochites (the *palimpsest* volume, which it is a wonder he did not recognize from the mutual comparison of the Leunclavian version as being indeed Glykas himself), the Capitoline Fasti prevail, which explicitly attribute the years to him. But falsely. For how many you will have counted from that first dictatorship initialized in the year U.C. 705, up to the Ides of March of the year 709, not yet finished, in which he perished, struck by many wounds in the Senate; you will have rightly declared that so many monarchs have existed.
you have given me the command to carry out my duties for the remainder of my life, as if to someone who is about to defile the marriages of others. Upon this, Athenodorus, on the point of returning to his homeland—knowing that the Caesar was a man of precipitous anger, but of a kind that would suddenly burn out—leaned toward his ear as if to kiss him, and said: "Caesar Augustus, one thing only is still wanting to you. Do not pass sentence upon the accused before you have recited the twenty-four letters to yourself." Moreover, Caesar was so beloved by his subjects that a certain Roman citizen, when about to depart from life, commanded his children to offer thank-offerings to the gods, because he left Augustus alive behind him. When Caesar himself was departing from life, he said: "I found the city earthen, I have left it adorned with beautiful towers and marble." Furthermore, he said that he wished for many to clap their hands for him when he died, as is accustomed to be done at the death of a mime or a player. These words, in truth, looked to nothing else than that he was mocking this life. And so much for these matters. Know, however, beloved son, that in the 42nd year of the reign of the aforementioned Augustus Caesar, our Lord Jesus Christ was born, according to the flesh, from the ever-virgin Mary. For this blameless woman was born according to a divine promise, and when she had reached her third year, she was led to the temple and spent a sufficient time in its inner sanctums, with her food provided by an angel, until she was fourteen years of age. Concerning this, the high priests, as was fitting, took counsel and offered prayers to God. At that time, she was betrothed to Joseph, God himself prompting this by means of the staff that sprouted. Then, indeed, the prophetic utterance received its fulfillment, which says: "Behold, a sealed book is given to a man who knows letters," (that is, to one who had lived in marriage), "and he shall say, I cannot read it, for it is sealed." Not much time passed before a divine angel was sent to her, saying: "Hail, full of grace, the Lord is with you." She, having become startled at the word, and saying, "How shall this be, since I know not a man?" hears from Gabriel these things: "The Holy Spirit will come upon you, and the power of the Most High will overshadow you." Observe that by saying "Holy Spirit" and "power of the Most High," by which Christ is signified (for Christ is the power of God), He revealed the whole holy Trinity in this. For even if the Son alone was incarnate, nevertheless the Father and the Holy Spirit were present with Him as He was being incarnate, according to the indivisibility of the consubstantiality, as the great Athanasius says. According to this, Paul also used to say that in Him dwells all the fullness of the Godhead bodily, that is, substantially.
saying: "I will defend this city for my own sake, and for the sake of my servant David." And with what long-suffering He cares for the lives of the living, and how... But how is Abraham called faithful, having said to God: "By what shall I know that I shall inherit it?"—namely, Palestine. The expression "by what" is not that of one who is disbelieving, but of one longing to learn the manner of the inheritance. For countless nations inhabited Palestine at that time. And because of this, God said after the sacrifice: "Know for certain that your seed will be sojourners in a land not their own, and they will afflict them for four hundred years; but in the fourth generation they shall return hither, for not yet have the sins of the Amorites reached their fullness." Besides, there were some among them who were devout; this is clear from both Melchisedech and Abimelech. And regarding these, let it be said in this manner regarding the present. Melchisedech, although spoken of in Scripture as being without father and mother, nevertheless drew his lineage from Sidon, the son of Egypt, who also founded Sidon. Having built a city on Mount Sion, Salem, and having reigned therein for thirteen years, he fell asleep, a just man and a virgin. This is the celebrated... Now Abraham, with Sarah, begat Isaac, and with Hagar, Ishmael; and Isaac, with Rebecca, [begat] Esau and Jacob, who, because of the impiety of Esau, migrated to Laban, his mother's brother, from whom he also took his daughters, Leah and Rachel, as wives. This Jacob, therefore, begat the twelve patriarchs, that is, the twelve tribes of Israel. It should be known that after the three men were entertained by Abraham, the two proceeded to the burning of the Sodomites, who also reached the dwelling of Lot. The licentious Sodomites, learning of this, arrived there, seeking the strangers in order to satisfy their unlawful acts. Lot went out to meet them to the uttermost, and being greatly pressured by them, delivered up his own daughter to that defiling usage, only so that no such disgrace should befall the strangers. But the wicked ones would not be persuaded, and seeking the strangers with violence, they fought with Lot. The strangers opened the doors of the gateway, and appearing to them, they consumed everyone with fire alone. Stunned at what had occurred, Lot fell on his face and besought them to know who they were. They said to him: "Take all who are with you, and depart from this place; for tomorrow all will be burned, and the place will be winnowed and destroyed because of the [too] small sexual immoralities of its inhabitants." Lot, doing so quickly, escaped to the mountain of Segor. And the Lord having rained fire, all were burned according to the prophecy of the angels. But the wife of Lot, being a woman and not fearing the command of the angels, and having turned to see what had happened, was immediately turned to stone and became a pillar. ...that the whole world was already lost, and no seed would be born to them anymore, they made their father drunk with wine, and he had relations with them, not knowing, because of the drunkenness, what had happened through him. When his uncle, the divine Abraham, learned of this from him, he was grieved and sent him to the regions of the river Nile to fetch three staves from it, as though to show that he was innocent, but in truth so that he might be devoured by wild beasts and thus expiate the sin. Lot, having run swiftly by divine economy, offered to Abraham three staves—that is, of cypress, of pine, and of cedar. And Abraham, marveling that he had been saved from the beasts unscathed, glorified God. Both went up with the staves to the mountain, and Abraham planted them on a rock, apart from one another, yet near, as if forming a triangle. He commanded Lot to go away and draw water from the Jordan and to water them daily; this he did. The mountain was about twenty-four miles from the Jordan. Within three months the staves sprouted. And Lot, having gone down, told this to Abraham. And having gone up to see what had occurred, they found that the three staves had united and had become one, though the roots were left behind remaining as they had been planted, separate. Abraham, seeing the event in the dry wood, fell and worshipped the Lord, and said: "This wood shall be for the sin, an absolution." The wood became a great tree, and it lasted even until the kingdom of Solomon. Abraham received assurance concerning the forgiveness of Lot's sin. When the temple of Jerusalem was being built, this tree was also cut down with the others, and having been hewn, it lay idle in the Temple, also by the economy of God. And when Christ and our God was about to suffer the saving passion for the world, He was crucified upon it by the Jews.
his formulation, so to speak, added: "male and female he made them," it clearly showed that the former formation was one thing, and this latter is another; the one representing the generosity of the Creator (for "according to the image of God he made him"), the other indicating the difference of forms ("male and female he made them"). This argument may seem burdensome, but only to those who follow their own reasoning. For to those who search the sacred writings of the Fathers, it is worthy of all acceptance, because it is also clear. And hear what divine Anastasius, wise in spiritual things, says. For he added the following to what was said before, wishing to clarify from many things what was being spoken: "The second creation is the formation of the male and the female added to the creature, the one which had been made according to the first, namely according to the image of God, in which there was no difference of forms, because in the divine and blessed image no such thing is conceived." If these things are so, it is reasonable to say that out of foreknowledge God gave such a construction to Adam from the beginning, if indeed such a difference of forms belongs to the second creation, which He necessarily brought in after the first, as has been said; for He was not ignorant of what would happen. And pay attention, if you will, to John of Damascus speaking about the first man: "Since the God who knows the future knew that he would fall into transgression and would be subject to corruption, for this reason He brings forth the female from him." So that if he had not fallen from the angelic life, this difference of forms would not have been added, because he who was made according to the image of God would not have needed it for the continuation of the race by succession, since he was destined to be multiplied like the angels, as has been discussed above. But we must return to the discourse, even if again. Regarding the serpent. The divine sentence toward the serpent after the transgression: "Upon thy breast and belly thou shalt go." Since thou hast deceived the heart and persuaded the belly to take food, "upon thy breast and belly thou shalt go." For He does not declare that the serpent will now walk upon its belly as if it had possessed feet before, as Josephus and the so-called "Little Genesis" say. But since, according to John the Gold-tongued [Chrysostom], it had from its upright posture such an opportunity, so that it could approach Eve’s ear and discourse about such things, it was most justly condemned to drag itself upon the ground. For even if it had been fashioned footless from the beginning, still, by certain circles of coils beneath, it was raised up and moved alonged upon its extended chest. Hence even now the serpent, often angered, raises itself and runs, coiling in circles. And having proceeded as far as to show the memory of its ancient gait, it is again cast down by the law of the sentence. See also, with understanding, that it is so burdensome for someone to be by himself—
barley. Then, the earth in Mesopotamia having split open, a mule came forth, signaling an incursion of enemies with a human voice. At that time, a certain eunuch named Nicetas, who held the highest position under Copronymus and knew nothing other than eating and drinking, was ordained patriarch. Once, while reading the Gospel in his cell, instead of saying “according to Matthew,” he uttered “according to Matthaion.” Whereupon one of those standing by said, “Do not divide the diphthong ‘ai.’” To him he replied with anger, “You are talking nonsense; my soul hates many diphthongs and triphthongs!” The tyrant, returning from an expedition, developed gangrene in his legs, and died near the Strougylon, seized by a violent fever, saying only this at the end: “Living, I am delivered to unquenchable fire because of Mary; but from now on, let her be honored.” Know, beloved, that having searched many times for why he was called Copronymus, we found nothing other than this: there is a region called Kabala. Those who originated from there were naturally called Kabalinoi. He himself had his birth from there, and since by a certain custom dung is called kabalina, that basest of men was justly nicknamed Copronymus, as though he were carrying the name of dung within himself. After him, his son Leo, from the Chazar, ruled for 5 years, being pious at the beginning. Afterward, he wore the ecclesiastical crown, from which he died, having been incinerated as if by a violent fever. At that time, that dog also existed which recognized the murderer of its master as he entered a tavern to drink wine. For after the murder of its master, the dog became a wayfarer accompanying a certain evildoer. The murderer, having chanced to enter there, was recognized by the dog, and the dog barked at him so much that it caused suspicion about him. The murderer was subsequently held, brought before the magistrate, examined strictly, confessed against his will, and was finally executed as an evildoer. After him, his son Constantine, with his mother Irene, ruled for 17 years, by whom piety was strengthened and monasteries were expanded. At that time also, someone excavating in Thrace found a sarcophagus on which was written: “Christ is about to be born of Mary the Virgin, and I believe in Him. In the time of Constantine and Irene, the pious emperors, you shall see me again, O sun.” This Irene, co-reigning with her son Constantine, and not bearing what was being done by him (for he had caused her to fall from power, although he repented and brought her back to the palace, and he cast out his own wife, who was chaste and very good, and introduced the union of another woman), for this reason blinded him while he was sleeping, so that from that time the sun did not give forth its pure rays for many days. But Empress Irene, having felt remorse for what she had done, and wishing to wash herself clean of the hatred, emptied the treasures of wealth into the city and resolved its debts. Nevertheless, the all-seeing eye did not slumber; for, having tasted the pleasure of power for a brief time, she drank a cup that tasted of wormwood. For one of the senators, by the name of Nicephorus, a patrician by title from the generals, rose up and dishonorably displaced her from the kingdom. This Nicephorus, therefore, ruled for 9 years, a man most murderous, a slave to gold, another Midas in every sense, making the offices available to those who would buy them. He seemed gloomy to everyone, but when they brought him gold, he appeared very charming. But he also imposed a heavy burden on the Byzantines, and because of the aging walls, he ordered the collection of the so-called “dikeron.” In addition to these things, the arrears owed to the army also prevailed. At that time, the relic of Saint Euphemia, which had been cast into the sea by Copronymus, was recovered on the island of Lesbos. And concerning these matters, thus it is. But Zonaras...
534 Being sent into exile, he stood upright and would offer a greeting to someone. Having been asked what this meant, and to whom he was offering the greeting, he would say it was to Theophanes the Confessor, the abbot of the Field, because he welcomed us with candles and incense. Upon these things, Leo, reflecting on the monk’s prediction regarding himself, kept Michael in bonds, since such a man was even more bold and shameless. Wherefore, a vote was taken by the senate for Michael to become fodder for the fire. This would have indeed come to pass, had the Augusta not prevented such a thing, perhaps putting forward as intercession the holy birth of Christ which was present at that time. Leo, however, being terrified by dreams and oracles and other such things, hastened his execution; but he also said to his spouse, “Michael is already being finished off because of you, and you and those from you will soon know why.” Not long after, Michael, emboldened by the prediction of the aforementioned monk, plotted an ambush against Leo, having revealed this even to Pappias, who was guarding him. Thus it was that by night he brought in armed soldiers together with the chanters. And Michael was raised to the imperial throne, though his feet were held by an iron shackle; but Leo, who had fled to the holy church, he cut down mercilessly with swords. Therefore, after Leo, the stammering Michael reigned for 8 years, he who originated from Amorium and was impious, yet he did not force the pious to be impious. Upon this, the saints were released at that time, and the great Theodore was brought back from his exile; for the Armenian had forestalled him by exiling him and doing many evils, inasmuch as he defended the holy icons. During the reign of this Michael, a certain Thomas, having gathered a heavy force from the East and the West, and having made not a few triremes, campaigned against the Queen of Cities. So great was his power that he reached the extent of breaking the chain that was suspended on the acropolis and went towards Blachernae. For three whole years, then, the matters of the war were beaten out. Then Thomas was defeated, fled, and came to Adrianople. He was kept under watch there in a circle by Michael for a sufficient time. He gave up, even though unwilling; he was seized, his hands and feet were cut off, he was set upon a donkey, saying only this: “Have mercy on me, O true emperor.” After Michael, his son Theophilus reigned for 12 years and 3 months. At that time, he made a selection of maidens, one of whom was Kasia, whom he sent away because of her answer full of wisdom; for having offered her an apple, as if he had fallen in love on account of her beauty, and saying, “From a woman flowed the base things,” he heard from her that from a woman also flowed the better things. Wherefore he took the Paphlagonian Theodora as his wife. Every week he would ride on horseback through the market to the church in Blachernae; for even if he did not pay honor to the holy icons, he kept his faith to the Savior and the Theotokos herself, as he used to say. He would also inquire about the goods for sale in the market, and if they were being sold for a high price, he would become angry at the Eparch. To him came a woman wronged by Petronas, the kinsman of the Augusta; for by raising a certain dwelling, he was harming her who was his neighbor. Petronas had indeed been spoken to, but he did not rectify it. Wherefore he was punished severely, and the dwelling having been torn down, all the material was given to the woman. When, having looked out, he saw the Empress’s cargo ship, he ordered it to be committed to the flames, saying, “The Lord having shown me to be emperor, you are forcing me to be a shipmaster? Besides, if we are hastening to acquire for ourselves things from commerce along with the royal prosperity, from where would private citizens procure the things for life?” So magnificent an emperor was Theophilus that he even fashioned some marvelous devices. For spirit, sent up from there in a certain mechanism, would echo music
that there was no other cause for the flood being brought on, the most divine Cyril clearly demonstrates this in the book against Julian, saying: “The human beings descended from Adam down to Noah honored one true God. But after the construction of the tower and the division of the languages, they held an incompatible opinion regarding God; for having been scattered everywhere, they possessed the delusion of polytheism.” That the universal deluge occurred because of the debauchery of both parties, God Himself indicated, saying: “My Spirit shall not remain in these men, because they are flesh,” that is to say, because they live passionately and carnally. Thus, therefore, the great Paul calls those who live temperately “not in the flesh,” saying: “But you are not in the flesh.” Indeed, it is the custom of Scripture to call them “flesh,” just as it also calls them “ungodly,” those who lead a profligate life; for ungodliness is not only the worship of idols, but also the very life that is lascivious and profligate. And the blessed David declares this, saying: “I said, I will confess my transgression against me to the Lord, and You forgave the ungodliness of my heart.” For was David an unbeliever? Did he entirely abjure God? No, but he called by the name of ungodliness the desire which gave birth to adultery. And God brought that universal flood because all those people were in need of a purgation. And Scripture presents this, saying: “And the Lord God saw the earth, and it was corrupted, because all flesh had corrupted its way upon the earth.” Pay attention, therefore, beloved. For if God did not spare anyone at that time, but drowned them all utterly, having gained nothing from their honoring God by words alone, it is surely evident that He will not spare us either on that day, even if we bear the name of Christians. For our Master Christ, the Lord of all, will truly say to us, that which He said to the Jews when they spoke boastfully: “We are from our father Abraham.” For just as He said to them, “If you were from Abraham, you would do the works of Abraham,” so He will also say to us: “If you considered yourselves Christians, you would do the works that are fitting for Christians.” Do not, therefore, doubt these things; for by that universal flood and the conflagration at Sodom, God clearly showed what kind of end awaits those who spend their lives in debauchery. Now Noah, having reached five hundred years, begat Shem, Ham, and Japheth. On account of which the man is worthy of admiration, that he passed over so much time, and did not wish to submit to even lawful unions. And as the Golden-mouthed says, if there had not been a divine dispensation,
Furthermore, that most evil custom of the Sodomites persuaded them to display their lewdness even toward the angels themselves; wherefore they also became the object of unquenchable fire. For those Sodoms, which stood so fair and beautiful, are in truth nothing but ash (for even the very air together with the waters there is set ablaze)—a clear token that their punishment continues eternally. These things concerning Palestine, Ascalon, and Gaza, lying above near the outlet of the Jordan, did not differ at that time at all from the paradise of old. Therefore, let he who does not believe in hell reflect upon Sodom, and thus let him flee gluttony; for such is it accustomed to generate such passions. In accordance with this, Moses also said to Israel: “When you have eaten and drunk, take heed to yourself, lest you forget the Lord your God.” Moreover, Galen, that most excellent of the art of medicine, shows that luxury itself is the cause of such passions. In our Mysia, which is adjacent to Pergamum, he says, one would rarely find a woman committing adultery, and even rarer to find lewd men accustomed to the most unspeakable amours, but in Rome, because of the prevailing and common luxury, these things are easy to commit. But if, as Ptolemy says, because some are affiliated with the first trigon of the zodiac—that is, the ram, the lion, and the archer—they do certain nefarious things, and think they do nothing abominable because they are under signs that are masculine in front but feminine behind, is God then unjust for having reduced Sodom to ashes by fire? But it is impossible that the Just One should ever be unjust. For such people, living in the abundance of bread as the Scripture says, and not being [so disposed] because of such a trigon, having rolled themselves into lawless beds, have reasonably become the fuel for fire. We learn, therefore, from this that on that day the righteous, just as Noah and Lot were here, will be found above these tortures, while sinners, even if they should be the entire world, will be subjected to almost eternal punishments, just as here all those who did the worst things were delivered to the flood and fire.
Now Abraham was called Abram at first, which is said to mean “a crosser”; for as it seems, his mother named him thus by divine providence, since he was to cross the Euphrates and dwell in Palestine. Abraham was seventy-five years old when he received the promises. He begat Ishmael when he was eighty-six years old. And when he had reached the age of ninety-nine, God appeared to him, saying: “I am God.” Then the righteous man was circumcised, serving as a canon set before all. And that the boldness of the righteous who are in the cities is the cause of God’s long-suffering toward us, you may learn from the very history of Sodom itself; for He says: “If I find ten righteous men, I will not destroy the cities.” And often, even because of the virtue of those who have departed, or even because of the world itself,
[Next page begins: λέγων· ο Υπερασπιῶ τῆς πόλεως ταύτης δι' ἐμὲ καὶ διὰ Δαβὶδ τὸν παϊδά μου. » κεία…]
is a certain kind of skill found in animals that possess neither reason nor the use of voice. For though you might scarcely find anyone who counts the dolphin among the great whales along with seals, whales, and the zygænae, know that whales observe the place prescribed to them by nature in which they dwell. For they occupy the sea that extends beyond the regions inhabited by mortals and lies beyond the islands; where no further continent remains that might be a hindrance to their dwelling. Nor does any history or usage encourage sailors to venture anything against them. Having followed [nature] to that sea, these whales—resembling the largest mountains in their magnitude, as those who have seen them relate—remain within their own limits, neither harming the islands nor approaching the coastal cities, but remaining in those parts of the sea that were assigned to them from the beginning. Among other whales, moreover, there is one called the "saw-fish": if it sees a ship billowing its sails, it imitates the ship. There is also another whale named the "shield-turtle" B which, when it is hungry, opens its mouth; and because a fragrant odor issues from it, smaller fish enter, and thus the beast is sated. We see something of this sort happen also with humans and the evil spirit. For he never ceases to pursue them and, having misled them in various ways, he ensnares them, especially those who are somewhat pusillanimous in spirit; for he devours them, lured by the pleasures and delights of life, although he is never sated by devouring. There are also other kinds of whales, seals, whales, dolphins, zygænae (or libellae), and other such creatures, regarding which this from the C Sacred Scriptures must be understood: "And God created the great whales," living in separately prescribed places, so that no one who encounters them might be harmed. "And God created the great whales." In these words, you should not think they are called great because they are larger than a shrimp or a herring, but because, on account of the bulk of their bodies, they can be compared to the largest mountains. Indeed, they often provide the appearance of islands whenever they swim upon the surface of the water. Being of such a size, they do not loiter along the shores or the beaches, but dwell in the Atlantic Ocean. And these animals were created for our fear and trembling. But D truly, when you hear that great vessels, which are carried with sails spread by a favorable wind, are brought to a stop so easily by the minute little fish, the echeneis, so as to hold the ship motionless for a long period of time as if it were fixed with roots in the very sea, do you not perceive the power of the Creator in this tiny animal as well? Surely, from all things, as much from the great as from the small, you must take arguments concerning the power of the Creator. What, I pray, happens to the hands of those who…
the fact that not one of the winged creatures lacks feet, for the reason that its sustenance is gained from the earth. And all necessarily [need them]; for to birds of prey, the sharpness of their claws is granted for catching their prey, while for the rest the service of their feet is provided for the conduct of their life. Few birds are weak-footed, being unfit either for walking or for hunting with their feet, such as the swallows and those called swifts, for whom food has been devised from things borne about in the air.
Mark this, then. For there is one construction for seed-eating birds, and another for the carnivorous, suited to the manner of their diet: the sharpness of claws, a hooked beak, and swift wings, so that the prey may be captured easily. For this reason, also, the claws of animals are harder than those of man, for the latter’s [hands] were made softer because his skin also is softer, from which the nails take their origin; and because the hardness and softness of nails were bestowed upon each of the animals according to their use. Furthermore, among winged creatures, some are leaderless and, as it were, autonomous, while others submit to being placed under a leader, such as the cranes. But also the bees perform their tasks under a king and a sort of battalion commander, nor do they consent to go out to the meadows until they see their king having initiated the flight. This [king] is not appointed by vote, but possesses from nature the primacy over all, distinguished in magnitude, in shape, and in the gentleness of his character. For the king has a sting, but he does not use it for vengeance. These are certain unwritten laws of nature: that those who attain the highest powers should be slow to punish. In this way, those [bees] that do not follow the example of the king die from the wound of the sting, finding the reward deserved by their own cruelty. It is also worthy of admiration how cranes undertake their night watches by rotation; while some sleep, others walk in a circle and provide them complete safety during their sleep. Then, when the time for the watch is fulfilled, the one that gave the cry betakes itself to sleep, and the one that received the turn, [repaying] the safety it [the former] chanced to enjoy, renders it back to the rest.
Thus, therefore, the behavior of the storks also brings everyone to astonishment, being no less than the things already mentioned. For they surround their father, who has shed his feathers through old age, circle him, and warm him with their own wings; and, providing food abundantly, they supply him with assistance in flying, gently lifting him on both sides with their wings. And this is so famous to all that from this event some have come to call the return of benefits “antipelargosis” (mutuality of the storks). Let no one, therefore, despair of his own life on account of poverty. And who would not be exceedingly moved by the ingenuity of the swallow
the water with this animal. And for this reason they stir up the waters with their trunks as they drink. There is also this other thing worthy of wonder in elephants. Whenever they want to cross the Indian river, they do not make the passage simply and as it happens, but the smallest of all is the first to begin the crossing, then another larger than it, then another still greater than both of them. And in sequence, using such order, they cross the river without hindrance. For if the larger ones were to begin the journey, perhaps by the weight of their feet they would create pits in the very mud of the riverbed, and would no longer allow those possessing the smallest bodies to proceed; nay rather, they would force such animals to be suffocated in the mire of the river. And that the elephant does not approach into pairing in any other way, as those who are expert in such matters say, unless it tastes the mandrake (for there is no inclination to copulation before this, where the female, having taken the mandrake and eaten of it, gives to the male, and thus they come to intercourse), is clearly referred to the first-created; for they, too, before the tasting of the plant, had a life equal to the angels, but after the tasting of the tree and the introduction of disobedience, *Adam knew Eve his wife and she brought forth Cain*. But what need is there even to speak of the ant-lion? For it is written in Job, *The ant-lion has perished for having no food*. The ant-lion is a certain animal begotten from an ant and a lion, but it soon meets its end because it does not find food appropriate to it; for as a lion it seeks meat, but as an ant it seeks grain, and for this reason it easily perishes. These things are all referred to the devil himself, who begins from small things but arrives at great ones. Concerning whom also one of the Fathers said: *The devil knows how to belittle the smallness of sins; for otherwise he could not bring you to a greater evil.* Whence he also perishes, and dies, so to speak, like the ant-lion, finding neither food nor rest from us.
Now the elephant, if it falls, is unable to rise because it does not have joints in its feet. Moreover, these creatures, even though they bear the largest bodies, are uniparous. For this very reason they ought properly to be multiparous—I speak of the elephant, the horse, and the camel; but since the nourishment is consumed for the growth of the body, they cannot feed a multitude of embryos. And concerning these, let this suffice. But they say that hares bring forth some of their offspring, while others they have brought forth, and others still incomplete are carried in the womb. The reason is that the nature of hares is most fruitful. For see that hare rennet placed upon a barren woman quickly makes her pregnant, and grants to a sterile woman a child to nurse. Thus conception is not fixed in hares, but while the offspring are still being carried, a super-conception occurs in them. Therefore, some that have been brought forth
Ephon’s son completely destroyed the three sons of Enac, namely Susem, Tholmeus, and Schimas; these were giants with bodies of extraordinary stature, for which reason they are called the sons of Enac in the Holy Scriptures.
It ought to be known that the first law among the Hebrews is written in Exodus as follows: “Thou shalt have no other gods before me. Thou shalt not make unto thee any graven image, nor any likeness of any thing that is in heaven above, or that is in the earth beneath.” There is no tautology here between the “graven image” (idolum) and the “likeness” (similitudo), for the likeness is of something—whether a terrestrial animal, a marine one, or a winged one. But the idol, according to Origen’s interpretation, is a construct of the mind that has no substantial existence, such as the cynocephalus honored by the Egyptians or the hippocentaur among the Greeks. Furthermore, you should know that the first law among the Hebrews was written in the book that concerns their exodus from Egypt; and these are the first words of the law: “Thou shalt have no other gods before me. Thou shalt not make to thyself a sculpture, nor an image of anything whatsoever that exists either above in heaven or below on earth.” There is no inept repetition of the same thing here, for an idol and an image do not signify the same thing; for an image is of either a land animal, or a sea animal, or a flying one, whereas an idol, according to the opinion and explanation of Origen, exists as an invention of the mind, just as that cynocephalus, which was held in religious veneration by the Egyptians, was a mental construct, or as the Hippocentaur of the Greeks.
314 After Jesus, the son of Nave, the people were governed by judges. The first of these was Judas who overcame Adonibezec and maimed his hands and feet, for he was a king of foreign nations. For the Canaanites, taking heart after the death of Jesus to overcome the Judeans, entrusted the leadership to Adonibezec and went out against them. But they were disappointed in their goal; for not a few of them were slain, and Adonibezec was captured; and having his hands and feet maimed, he said: “Seventy kings, with the extremities of their hands and feet cut off, gathered the scraps under my table. As I have done, so has God repaid me.”
But also Barach, together with the prophetess Debbora, judged Israel for forty years. Debbora was a prophetess. She kept the Judeans—who wished to retreat because of the multitude of the Canaanites who had mobilized against them—and persuaded them to engage the enemy; hence they killed many of them. For a heavy winter having occurred harmed the Canaanites greatly, but the Judeans not at all. Then also Sisaras, seeing the rout that had occurred, leaped from his chariot and fled to a certain woman named Jael, so that he might be saved by her. And to this Sisaras, asking for a drink, she gave milk that was already starting to spoil. And having drunk more than the measure, he fell into a deep sleep. Then indeed Jael slew him by driving a peg through his temples. And thus victory was theirs according to the prophecy of Debbora. Thus the Scripture rightly called Susem, Tholmeus, and Schimas the sons of Enac, giants.
The male pig has tusks, as it is sharper for striking, while the female does not have them, and so it bites in defense. So too with deer: the male bears antlers, but the female does not. Moreover, those that are castrated do not have tusks, because their nature changes toward the feminine; for the females of these are deprived of them. Know also this, that wild pigs, which use simple nourishment, give birth once a year, while tame ones give birth twice because of their rich diet. When they are fattened, they lose their hair and bristles; for as they become fat, they become fleshy—or rather, such are those that digest well—and those that digest well lack waste matter, and the hair comes from the waste matter. The reason why the pig and the horse carry foam at the mouth is this: heat and motion are accustomed to bleach liquids. And see the sea: it is whitened when moved by the winds. Look at pots placed over a fire. Look at the humidity in the air: in winter it is whitened when moved by the winds, and once whitened, it freezes due to the cold at that time, and thus snow is brought down; for snow is nothing else but semi-frozen water. So then, in the pig and the horse, humors, thinned into bubbles by heat and motion, are transformed into foam. But if you also wish to know the reason why dogs alone go mad during the summer, here it is clearly set before you. Dogs go mad because of the accumulation of a dry temperament. They are dry by nature, and more so during heat waves. Therefore, the humors within them, heating up and drying out, become inflamed, and thence, because of excessive dryness, they go mad, just like those suffering from frenzy. Whence also their saliva becomes poisonous and drier. That they are inflamed is clear from their frequent panting, from having fiery eyes, and an open mouth for the cooling of the air. Not all dogs go mad, but as many of them as are drier. Some also say that the star, the Dog, contributes to this madness by some sort of emanation. Having gone mad, as Paul the Sinopian says, they turn away from both food and drink, and they let out copious and foamy saliva, and they are altogether voiceless, and moreover senseless, so that they do not even recognize their own masters. They rush upon everyone without barking and bite, and when they bite, they do not cause anything troublesome immediately, except for the pain from the wound. Later, however, they produce the affliction called hydrophobia; for those bitten by rabid dogs flee from water, both when they see it and when it is brought to them. Some of them also, as... Sinopensis. "κτῆνος, καὶ τελευτῶν αὐτίκα οἱ βάτραχοι, κἂν ὁ Φαραὼ θεασάμενος ὅτι γέγονεν ἄνεσις, ἐπαίρεται καὶ πάλιν εἶναι σκληρός. Τρίτη δὲ πληγὴ ἡ τῶν σκινιφῶν ἐπὶ τὴν γῆν Αἰγύπτου γένεσις. Ὁ γὰρ Κύριος εἶπε πρὸς Μωσέα · « Εἰπὸν Ἀαρὼν, ἔκτεινον τὴν χεῖρα, καὶ πάταξον τῇ βακτηρίᾳ τὸν χοῦν τῆς γῆς, καὶ ἔσται σκινίφες ἐν τοῖς ἀνθρώποις καὶ ἐν τοῖς τετράποσιν. » Ἐποίησε δὲ ταὐτὰ Ἀαρών, καὶ οἱ ἐπαοιδοὶ οὐκ ἠδυνήθησαν, καὶ εἶπον · « Δάκτυλος Θεοῦ ἐστι τοῦτο. » Ὁρᾷς ὅτι ἀσθενῆ τὰ τῶν μάγων, καὶ ἀδυνατοῦντα πρὸς τὰ θεῖα καὶ ἄκτιστα; Τετάρτη δὲ πληγὴ ἡ τῶν κυνυμιῶν. « Κἀγὼ , φησὶν ὁ Κύριος, παταξω ἐν σοὶ καὶ ἐν τοῖς θεράπουσί σου καὶ ἐν τῷ λαῷ σου κυνυμιὰν. » Ἐπισημήνασθαι δὲ δεῖ, ὅτι ἐν ταύτῃ τῇ πληγῇ διεχώρισε ὁ Θεὸς τὴν γῆν Γεσὲμ, ἐν ᾗ ἦσαν οἱ υἱοὶ Ἰσραήλ, ἀπὸ τῆς γῆς Αἰγύπτου, ἵνα μὴ ἐκείνοις συμπαθῶσι τῆς πληγῆς οἱ Ἰσραηλῖται. Πέμπτη δὲ πληγὴ ὁ θάνατος τῶν κτηνῶν Αἰγυπτίων. « Ἰδοὺ , φησὶν, ἡ χεὶρ Κυρίου ἐπὶ τὰ κτήνη τὰ ἐν τοῖς πεδίοις, καὶ ἔσται θάνατος μέγας σφόδρα. » Ἕκτη δὲ πληγὴ τὰ ἐλκή. « Λάβετε, φησὶν, ὑμῖν πλήρη τὰς χεῖρας αἰθάλης καμιναίας, καὶ πασπάσει αὐτὴν Μωσῆς εἰς τὸν οὐρανὸν ἐναντίον Φαραὼ, καὶ ἔσται κονιορτὸς ἐπὶ πᾶσαν τὴν γῆν Αἰγύπτου, καὶ ἔσται ἐπὶ τοὺς ἀνθρώπους καὶ ἐπὶ τὰ κτήνη ἕλκη φλυκτίδες ἀναζέουσαι. » Ἑβδόμη δὲ πληγὴ ἡ τῆς χαλάζης καὶ τοῦ πυρὸς ἐπιφορὰ, καὶ ὅτι τῇ χαλάζῃ πῦρ ἐμίγνυτο. Ὀγδόη δὲ πληγὴ ἡ τῶν ἀκρίδων ἐπίθεσις. Ἔνατη δὲ πληγὴ τὸ σκότος τὸ ψηλαφητόν. Δεκάτη δὲ πληγὴ ὁ θάνατος τῶν πρωτοτόκων Αἰγυπτίων. ΤΜΗΜΑ ΔΕΥΤΕΡΟΝ PARS II. AB ADAMO USQUE AD CÆSARIS IMPERIUM. Ταῦτα μὲν οὖν εἰ καὶ παρεκβατικώτερον εἴρηται, ἀλλὰ φιλομαθείας ἕνεκα πάντως τῆς σῆς. Ἐφέσει γὰρ, ὡς ὁρᾷς, τῇ σῇ χαριζόμενος, καὶ φιλοπόνως ταῦτα συνέλεξα καὶ φιλοτίμως, τῇδε διείληφα τῷ συντάγματι. cattle; and then the frogs immediately died, and even if Pharaoh, seeing that there was a respite, became arrogant and stubborn again. The third plague was the origin of the gnats throughout the land of Egypt. For the Lord said to Moses: "Say to Aaron, stretch out your hand, and strike the dust of the earth with your staff, and there shall be gnats on the men and on the four-footed beasts." Aaron did these things, and the enchanters could not, and they said: "It is the finger of God." Do you see that the works of the magicians are weak and powerless before divine and uncreated things? The fourth plague was that of the dog-flies. "And I," says the Lord, "will send on you and on your servants and on your people a dog-fly." And it is necessary to point out that in this plague God separated the land of Goshen, in which the sons of Israel were, from the land of Egypt, so that the Israelites might not suffer because of the plague along with them. The fifth plague was the death of the Egyptian livestock. "Behold," he says, "the hand of the Lord is upon the livestock that are in the fields, and there shall be a very great death." The sixth plague was the boils. "Take for yourselves," he says, "full handfuls of furnace soot, and Moses shall sprinkle it toward heaven before Pharaoh, and it shall be dust upon all the land of Egypt, and there shall be boils breaking out into blisters upon the men and upon the livestock." The seventh plague was the sending of hail and fire, and that fire was mingled with the hail. The eighth plague was the attack of the locusts. The ninth plague was the darkness that could be felt. The tenth plague was the death of the firstborn of the Egyptians. PART II FROM ADAM UNTO THE EMPIRE OF CÆSAR These things, therefore, even if they have been said somewhat digressively, have been said certainly for the sake of your love of learning. For, as you see, yielding to your desire, I have laboriously and ambitiously collected these things, and have thus divided them in this treatise.
